From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—thanks. I guess I’m here because the doctor who did my insurance physical said my weight was lower than it should be. I don’t know, I’ve always thought I just looked kind of lean from work, being on ladders and moving around all day, long days and all that. But they seemed to think I should get it checked out, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of strong, I guess. But yeah, I do get pretty tense if the scale goes up even a little. Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I don’t really see it as unhealthy, exactly. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. I know that might sound like I’m dodging it.\n\nWhen I see myself in a mirror, or in photos next to one of my murals, I’m usually looking at whether my stomach looks flat enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp… defined, I guess. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think what would count as “large.” Like, I might eat more than I planned at a dinner or something and then feel kind of uncomfortable about it, but not like… a lot of food in an hour, the way you’re describing. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt guilty or uncomfortable after eating more than I meant to, but I don’t do that. I know that’s probably something you have to ask, but… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a planned way, like “I ate this so now I have to burn it off.” I mean, my job can be pretty physical, and sometimes I’ll take a walk or try to keep moving if I feel kind of heavy after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising specifically to cancel out calories.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. But it probably matters more to me than I want it to.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll end up judging the whole day by whether my stomach and my face look sharp enough. If they don’t, it just… it gets in my head. I know that probably isn’t the right way to measure things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight even if everyone around you told you that you looked healthy and didn't need to lose any more weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I would listen to them… especially if people were saying it in a caring way. But if I’m being honest, I don’t know that it would stop me.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or if I had bread or something, I get really tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, but it’s hard for me to just trust that “healthy” means I don’t need to do anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. Like, I don’t look in the mirror and think, “I’m heavier than I really am,” if that makes sense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—thanks. I guess I’m here because the doctor who did my insurance physical said my weight was lower than it should be. I don’t know, I’ve always thought I just looked kind of lean from work, being on ladders and moving around all day, long days and all that. But they seemed to think I should get it checked out, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of strong, I guess. But yeah, I do get pretty tense if the scale goes up even a little. Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I don’t really see it as unhealthy, exactly. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. I know that might sound like I’m dodging it.\n\nWhen I see myself in a mirror, or in photos next to one of my murals, I’m usually looking at whether my stomach looks flat enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp… defined, I guess. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think what would count as “large.” Like, I might eat more than I planned at a dinner or something and then feel kind of uncomfortable about it, but not like… a lot of food in an hour, the way you’re describing. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt guilty or uncomfortable after eating more than I meant to, but I don’t do that. I know that’s probably something you have to ask, but… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a planned way, like “I ate this so now I have to burn it off.” I mean, my job can be pretty physical, and sometimes I’ll take a walk or try to keep moving if I feel kind of heavy after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising specifically to cancel out calories.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. But it probably matters more to me than I want it to.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll end up judging the whole day by whether my stomach and my face look sharp enough. If they don’t, it just… it gets in my head. I know that probably isn’t the right way to measure things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight even if everyone around you told you that you looked healthy and didn't need to lose any more weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I would listen to them… especially if people were saying it in a caring way. But if I’m being honest, I don’t know that it would stop me.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or if I had bread or something, I get really tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, but it’s hard for me to just trust that “healthy” means I don’t need to do anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. Like, I don’t look in the mirror and think, “I’m heavier than I really am,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck on certain parts—my stomach, my face—and whether they look sharp enough. If I catch myself in a mirror, or in a photo next to one of my murals, it can kind of decide how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably sounds a little much… but that’s more what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is distorted when you look in the mirror, different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely different from what other people see, because that sounds dramatic.\n\nBut when I catch myself in a mirror, or if I see a photo of me next to one of my murals, I really focus on my stomach and my face. I don’t think it’s like… wanting to be as thin as humanly possible, exactly. That sounds extreme, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

But yeah, I do get pretty tense if the scale goes up even a little. Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I don’t really see it as unhealthy, exactly. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. I know that might sound like I’m dodging it.\n\nWhen I see myself in a mirror, or in photos next to one of my murals, I’m usually looking at whether my stomach looks flat enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp… defined, I guess. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. That sounds extreme, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more that if my weight goes up even a little, I feel really unsettled. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve had bread or something, I’ll check and then get tense if the number is higher. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that’s probably not the healthiest way to handle it… but it feels like I need to get it back under control.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would feel anxious or upset if you couldn't maintain your current body shape?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / painting_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\nmanifestation: \"If the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or something with bread, I get tense and start cutting back the next day so it does not stick.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Yeah… I think I would, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’d completely fall apart or anything, but if I felt like I couldn’t keep my shape where it is, I’d get pretty anxious.

I don’t know, I’ve always thought I just looked kind of lean from work, being on ladders and moving around all day, long days and all that. But they seemed to think I should get it checked out, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of strong, I guess. But yeah, I do get pretty tense if the scale goes up even a little. Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I don’t really see it as unhealthy, exactly. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. I know that might sound like I’m dodging it.\n\nWhen I see myself in a mirror, or in photos next to one of my murals, I’m usually looking at whether my stomach looks flat enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp… defined, I guess. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think what would count as “large.” Like, I might eat more than I planned at a dinner or something and then feel kind of uncomfortable about it, but not like… a lot of food in an hour, the way you’re describing. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt guilty or uncomfortable after eating more than I meant to, but I don’t do that. I know that’s probably something you have to ask, but… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a planned way, like “I ate this so now I have to burn it off.” I mean, my job can be pretty physical, and sometimes I’ll take a walk or try to keep moving if I feel kind of heavy after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising specifically to cancel out calories.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. But it probably matters more to me than I want it to.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll end up judging the whole day by whether my stomach and my face look sharp enough. If they don’t, it just… it gets in my head. I know that probably isn’t the right way to measure things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight even if everyone around you told you that you looked healthy and didn't need to lose any more weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I would listen to them… especially if people were saying it in a caring way. But if I’m being honest, I don’t know that it would stop me.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or if I had bread or something, I get really tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, but it’s hard for me to just trust that “healthy” means I don’t need to do anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. Like, I don’t look in the mirror and think, “I’m heavier than I really am,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck on certain parts—my stomach, my face—and whether they look sharp enough. If I catch myself in a mirror, or in a photo next to one of my murals, it can kind of decide how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably sounds a little much… but that’s more what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is distorted when you look in the mirror, different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely different from what other people see, because that sounds dramatic.\n\nBut when I catch myself in a mirror, or if I see a photo of me next to one of my murals, I really focus on my stomach and my face. Like whether they look sharp enough. And if they don’t, it can kind of ruin how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably isn’t a normal amount to put on one reflection.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to be as thin as possible, or is it more about not wanting to feel out of control with your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like… wanting to be as thin as humanly possible, exactly. I know that’s probably not the healthiest way to handle it… but it feels like I need to get it back under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or upset if you couldn't maintain your current body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’d completely fall apart or anything, but if I felt like I couldn’t keep my shape where it is, I’d get pretty anxious.\n\nLike if the scale is up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it. It’s not just the exact number, like I’m chasing some specific weight. It’s more that if the scale goes up even a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had bread—I get this tense feeling, like I’ve let something slip.

And then I’ll usually cut back the next day, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that might sound a little overcontrolled, but… yeah, it feels more like needing to get my body back under control than just not wanting one particular number.

But yeah, I do get pretty tense if the scale goes up even a little. Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I don’t really see it as unhealthy, exactly. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think what would count as “large.” Like, I might eat more than I planned at a dinner or something and then feel kind of uncomfortable about it, but not like… a lot of food in an hour, the way you’re describing. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt guilty or uncomfortable after eating more than I meant to, but I don’t do that. I know that’s probably something you have to ask, but… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a planned way, like “I ate this so now I have to burn it off.” I mean, my job can be pretty physical, and sometimes I’ll take a walk or try to keep moving if I feel kind of heavy after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising specifically to cancel out calories.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. But it probably matters more to me than I want it to.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll end up judging the whole day by whether my stomach and my face look sharp enough. If they don’t, it just… it gets in my head. I know that probably isn’t the right way to measure things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight even if everyone around you told you that you looked healthy and didn't need to lose any more weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I would listen to them… especially if people were saying it in a caring way. But if I’m being honest, I don’t know that it would stop me.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or if I had bread or something, I get really tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, but it’s hard for me to just trust that “healthy” means I don’t need to do anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. Like, I don’t look in the mirror and think, “I’m heavier than I really am,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck on certain parts—my stomach, my face—and whether they look sharp enough. If I catch myself in a mirror, or in a photo next to one of my murals, it can kind of decide how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably sounds a little much… but that’s more what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is distorted when you look in the mirror, different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely different from what other people see, because that sounds dramatic.\n\nBut when I catch myself in a mirror, or if I see a photo of me next to one of my murals, I really focus on my stomach and my face. Like whether they look sharp enough. And if they don’t, it can kind of ruin how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably isn’t a normal amount to put on one reflection.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to be as thin as possible, or is it more about not wanting to feel out of control with your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like… wanting to be as thin as humanly possible, exactly. That sounds extreme, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more that if my weight goes up even a little, I feel really unsettled. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve had bread or something, I’ll check and then get tense if the number is higher. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that’s probably not the healthiest way to handle it… but it feels like I need to get it back under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or upset if you couldn't maintain your current body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’d completely fall apart or anything, but if I felt like I couldn’t keep my shape where it is, I’d get pretty anxious.\n\nLike if the scale is up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense. Then the next day I usually try to cut back so it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that might sound a little overcontrolled, but… yeah, it feels more like needing to get my body back under control than just not wanting one particular number.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you didn't care about how your body looks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / painting_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"When I catch myself in a mirror or in photos next to one of my murals, I judge the whole day by whether my stomach and face look sharp enough.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I’d like to say it wouldn’t matter if I didn’t care how I looked. That would probably be the healthier answer, right?

But honestly, a lot of it does come from seeing myself. Like if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll kind of judge how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach and face look… sharp enough, I guess. Not in a vain way, or at least I don’t think so, but it really affects me.

So… if I truly didn’t care how my body looked, maybe the need to control it wouldn’t be as strong. But right now, yeah, the way I look is definitely tied into it.

Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. I know that might sound like I’m dodging it.\n\nWhen I see myself in a mirror, or in photos next to one of my murals, I’m usually looking at whether my stomach looks flat enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp… defined, I guess. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think what would count as “large.” Like, I might eat more than I planned at a dinner or something and then feel kind of uncomfortable about it, but not like… a lot of food in an hour, the way you’re describing. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt guilty or uncomfortable after eating more than I meant to, but I don’t do that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. If they don’t, it just… it gets in my head. I know that probably isn’t the right way to measure things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight even if everyone around you told you that you looked healthy and didn't need to lose any more weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I would listen to them… especially if people were saying it in a caring way. But if I’m being honest, I don’t know that it would stop me.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or if I had bread or something, I get really tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, but it’s hard for me to just trust that “healthy” means I don’t need to do anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. If I catch myself in a mirror, or in a photo next to one of my murals, it can kind of decide how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably sounds a little much… but that’s more what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is distorted when you look in the mirror, different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely different from what other people see, because that sounds dramatic.\n\nBut when I catch myself in a mirror, or if I see a photo of me next to one of my murals, I really focus on my stomach and my face. Like whether they look sharp enough. And if they don’t, it can kind of ruin how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably isn’t a normal amount to put on one reflection.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to be as thin as possible, or is it more about not wanting to feel out of control with your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like… wanting to be as thin as humanly possible, exactly. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve had bread or something, I’ll check and then get tense if the number is higher. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that’s probably not the healthiest way to handle it… but it feels like I need to get it back under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or upset if you couldn't maintain your current body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’d completely fall apart or anything, but if I felt like I couldn’t keep my shape where it is, I’d get pretty anxious.\n\nLike if the scale is up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense. I know that probably sounds a bit rigid, but it’s more about feeling like I need to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about feeling like you're losing control over your body, rather than just not wanting to be a certain weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it. It’s not just the exact number, like I’m chasing some specific weight. It’s more that if the scale goes up even a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had bread—I get this tense feeling, like I’ve let something slip.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually cut back the next day, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that might sound a little overcontrolled, but… yeah, it feels more like needing to get my body back under control than just not wanting one particular number.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you didn't care about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say it wouldn’t matter if I didn’t care how I looked. That would probably be the healthier answer, right?\n\nBut honestly, a lot of it does come from seeing myself. Like if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll kind of judge how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach and face look… sharp enough, I guess. Not in a vain way, or at least I don’t think so, but it really affects me.\n\nSo… if I truly didn’t care how my body looked, maybe the need to control it wouldn’t be as strong. But right now, yeah, the way I look is definitely tied into it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your weight or body shape would still be a concern for you if you couldn't see yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / painting_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"When I catch myself in a mirror or in photos next to one of my murals, I judge the whole day by whether my stomach and face look sharp enough.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… maybe it would be less intense if I couldn’t actually see myself, yeah.

But I don’t know if it would go away completely. A lot of it starts when I catch myself in a mirror, or if there’s a photo of me next to one of my murals. I’ll sort of judge the whole day by whether my stomach and face look sharp enough. Which sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud, but that’s usually what happens.

So if mirrors and photos weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t get set off as much… but I think the concern would still be there in the background.

I don’t know, I’ve always thought I just looked kind of lean from work, being on ladders and moving around all day, long days and all that. But they seemed to think I should get it checked out, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of strong, I guess. But yeah, I do get pretty tense if the scale goes up even a little. Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I don’t really see it as unhealthy, exactly. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. I know that might sound like I’m dodging it.\n\nWhen I see myself in a mirror, or in photos next to one of my murals, I’m usually looking at whether my stomach looks flat enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp… defined, I guess. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think what would count as “large.” Like, I might eat more than I planned at a dinner or something and then feel kind of uncomfortable about it, but not like… a lot of food in an hour, the way you’re describing. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt guilty or uncomfortable after eating more than I meant to, but I don’t do that. I know that’s probably something you have to ask, but… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a planned way, like “I ate this so now I have to burn it off.” I mean, my job can be pretty physical, and sometimes I’ll take a walk or try to keep moving if I feel kind of heavy after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising specifically to cancel out calories.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. But it probably matters more to me than I want it to.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll end up judging the whole day by whether my stomach and my face look sharp enough. If they don’t, it just… it gets in my head. I know that probably isn’t the right way to measure things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight even if everyone around you told you that you looked healthy and didn't need to lose any more weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I would listen to them… especially if people were saying it in a caring way. But if I’m being honest, I don’t know that it would stop me.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or if I had bread or something, I get really tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, but it’s hard for me to just trust that “healthy” means I don’t need to do anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. Like, I don’t look in the mirror and think, “I’m heavier than I really am,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck on certain parts—my stomach, my face—and whether they look sharp enough. If I catch myself in a mirror, or in a photo next to one of my murals, it can kind of decide how I feel about the whole day. I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely different from what other people see, because that sounds dramatic.\n\nBut when I catch myself in a mirror, or if I see a photo of me next to one of my murals, I really focus on my stomach and my face. Like whether they look sharp enough. And if they don’t, it can kind of ruin how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably isn’t a normal amount to put on one reflection.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to be as thin as possible, or is it more about not wanting to feel out of control with your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like… wanting to be as thin as humanly possible, exactly. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve had bread or something, I’ll check and then get tense if the number is higher. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. Then the next day I usually try to cut back so it doesn’t… stick, I guess. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… probably, yeah. Maybe it would be a little easier if I saw myself more accurately, and I want to say it would fix it, but I’m not sure it would.

Because even when it’s just the scale going up a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had something with bread—I get this tense feeling right away. Then I sort of… compensate the next day, cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. Not in some extreme way, I don’t think, but enough that it’s hard to ignore.

So I guess the fear would still be there, even if my perception was better. Does that make sense?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—thanks. I guess I’m here because the doctor who did my insurance physical said my weight was lower than it should be. I don’t know, I’ve always thought I just looked kind of lean from work, being on ladders and moving around all day, long days and all that. But they seemed to think I should get it checked out, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of strong, I guess. But yeah, I do get pretty tense if the scale goes up even a little. Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I don’t really see it as unhealthy, exactly. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. I know that might sound like I’m dodging it.\n\nWhen I see myself in a mirror, or in photos next to one of my murals, I’m usually looking at whether my stomach looks flat enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp… defined, I guess. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think what would count as “large.” Like, I might eat more than I planned at a dinner or something and then feel kind of uncomfortable about it, but not like… a lot of food in an hour, the way you’re describing. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt guilty or uncomfortable after eating more than I meant to, but I don’t do that. I know that’s probably something you have to ask, but… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a planned way, like “I ate this so now I have to burn it off.” I mean, my job can be pretty physical, and sometimes I’ll take a walk or try to keep moving if I feel kind of heavy after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising specifically to cancel out calories.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. But it probably matters more to me than I want it to.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll end up judging the whole day by whether my stomach and my face look sharp enough. If they don’t, it just… it gets in my head. I know that probably isn’t the right way to measure things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight even if everyone around you told you that you looked healthy and didn't need to lose any more weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I would listen to them… especially if people were saying it in a caring way. But if I’m being honest, I don’t know that it would stop me.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or if I had bread or something, I get really tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, but it’s hard for me to just trust that “healthy” means I don’t need to do anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. Like, I don’t look in the mirror and think, “I’m heavier than I really am,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck on certain parts—my stomach, my face—and whether they look sharp enough. If I catch myself in a mirror, or in a photo next to one of my murals, it can kind of decide how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably sounds a little much… but that’s more what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is distorted when you look in the mirror, different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely different from what other people see, because that sounds dramatic.\n\nBut when I catch myself in a mirror, or if I see a photo of me next to one of my murals, I really focus on my stomach and my face. Like whether they look sharp enough. And if they don’t, it can kind of ruin how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably isn’t a normal amount to put on one reflection.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to be as thin as possible, or is it more about not wanting to feel out of control with your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like… wanting to be as thin as humanly possible, exactly. That sounds extreme, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more that if my weight goes up even a little, I feel really unsettled. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve had bread or something, I’ll check and then get tense if the number is higher. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that’s probably not the healthiest way to handle it… but it feels like I need to get it back under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or upset if you couldn't maintain your current body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’d completely fall apart or anything, but if I felt like I couldn’t keep my shape where it is, I’d get pretty anxious.\n\nLike if the scale is up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense. Then the next day I usually try to cut back so it doesn’t… stick, I guess. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—thanks. I guess I’m here because the doctor who did my insurance physical said my weight was lower than it should be. I don’t know, I’ve always thought I just looked kind of lean from work, being on ladders and moving around all day, long days and all that. But they seemed to think I should get it checked out, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of strong, I guess. But yeah, I do get pretty tense if the scale goes up even a little. Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I don’t really see it as unhealthy, exactly. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. I know that might sound like I’m dodging it.\n\nWhen I see myself in a mirror, or in photos next to one of my murals, I’m usually looking at whether my stomach looks flat enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp… defined, I guess. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think what would count as “large.” Like, I might eat more than I planned at a dinner or something and then feel kind of uncomfortable about it, but not like… a lot of food in an hour, the way you’re describing. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt guilty or uncomfortable after eating more than I meant to, but I don’t do that. I know that’s probably something you have to ask, but… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a planned way, like “I ate this so now I have to burn it off.” I mean, my job can be pretty physical, and sometimes I’ll take a walk or try to keep moving if I feel kind of heavy after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising specifically to cancel out calories.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. But it probably matters more to me than I want it to.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll end up judging the whole day by whether my stomach and my face look sharp enough. If they don’t, it just… it gets in my head. I know that probably isn’t the right way to measure things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight even if everyone around you told you that you looked healthy and didn't need to lose any more weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I would listen to them… especially if people were saying it in a caring way. But if I’m being honest, I don’t know that it would stop me.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or if I had bread or something, I get really tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, but it’s hard for me to just trust that “healthy” means I don’t need to do anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. Like, I don’t look in the mirror and think, “I’m heavier than I really am,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck on certain parts—my stomach, my face—and whether they look sharp enough. If I catch myself in a mirror, or in a photo next to one of my murals, it can kind of decide how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably sounds a little much… but that’s more what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is distorted when you look in the mirror, different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely different from what other people see, because that sounds dramatic.\n\nBut when I catch myself in a mirror, or if I see a photo of me next to one of my murals, I really focus on my stomach and my face. Like whether they look sharp enough. And if they don’t, it can kind of ruin how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably isn’t a normal amount to put on one reflection.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to be as thin as possible, or is it more about not wanting to feel out of control with your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like… wanting to be as thin as humanly possible, exactly. That sounds extreme, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more that if my weight goes up even a little, I feel really unsettled. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve had bread or something, I’ll check and then get tense if the number is higher. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that’s probably not the healthiest way to handle it… but it feels like I need to get it back under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or upset if you couldn't maintain your current body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’d completely fall apart or anything, but if I felt like I couldn’t keep my shape where it is, I’d get pretty anxious.\n\nLike if the scale is up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense. Then the next day I usually try to cut back so it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds a bit rigid, but it’s more about feeling like I need to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about feeling like you're losing control over your body, rather than just not wanting to be a certain weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it. It’s not just the exact number, like I’m chasing some specific weight. It’s more that if the scale goes up even a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had bread—I get this tense feeling, like I’ve let something slip.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually cut back the next day, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that might sound a little overcontrolled, but… yeah, it feels more like needing to get my body back under control than just not wanting one particular number.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you didn't care about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say it wouldn’t matter if I didn’t care how I looked. That would probably be the healthier answer, right?\n\nBut honestly, a lot of it does come from seeing myself. Like if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll kind of judge how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach and face look… sharp enough, I guess. Not in a vain way, or at least I don’t think so, but it really affects me.\n\nSo… if I truly didn’t care how my body looked, maybe the need to control it wouldn’t be as strong. But right now, yeah, the way I look is definitely tied into it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape would still be a concern for you if you couldn't see yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it would be less intense if I couldn’t actually see myself, yeah.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would go away completely. A lot of it starts when I catch myself in a mirror, or if there’s a photo of me next to one of my murals. I’ll sort of judge the whole day by whether my stomach and face look sharp enough. Which sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nSo if mirrors and photos weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t get set off as much… but I think the concern would still be there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you had a more accurate perception of your body shape and weight?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. Maybe it would be a little easier if I saw myself more accurately, and I want to say it would fix it, but I’m not sure it would.\n\nBecause even when it’s just the scale going up a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had something with bread—I get this tense feeling right away. Then I sort of… compensate the next day, cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. Not in some extreme way, I don’t think, but enough that it’s hard to ignore.\n\nSo I guess the fear would still be there, even if my perception was better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight would decrease if you had a more realistic view of your body shape?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yeah. I think if I could see myself more realistically, it might take some of the edge off.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely stop the fear. Like, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense pretty fast. Then the next day I’ll usually cut back a bit, just to make sure it doesn’t… stay. I know that probably sounds more intense than I mean it to, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve had bread or something, I’ll check and then get tense if the number is higher. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that’s probably not the healthiest way to handle it… but it feels like I need to get it back under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or upset if you couldn't maintain your current body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’d completely fall apart or anything, but if I felt like I couldn’t keep my shape where it is, I’d get pretty anxious.\n\nLike if the scale is up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense. Then the next day I usually try to cut back so it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds a bit rigid, but it’s more about feeling like I need to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about feeling like you're losing control over your body, rather than just not wanting to be a certain weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it. It’s not just the exact number, like I’m chasing some specific weight. It’s more that if the scale goes up even a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had bread—I get this tense feeling, like I’ve let something slip.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually cut back the next day, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that might sound a little overcontrolled, but… yeah, it feels more like needing to get my body back under control than just not wanting one particular number.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you didn't care about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say it wouldn’t matter if I didn’t care how I looked. Like if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll kind of judge how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach and face look… sharp enough, I guess. Not in a vain way, or at least I don’t think so, but it really affects me.\n\nSo… if I truly didn’t care how my body looked, maybe the need to control it wouldn’t be as strong. But right now, yeah, the way I look is definitely tied into it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape would still be a concern for you if you couldn't see yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it would be less intense if I couldn’t actually see myself, yeah.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would go away completely. A lot of it starts when I catch myself in a mirror, or if there’s a photo of me next to one of my murals. I’ll sort of judge the whole day by whether my stomach and face look sharp enough. Which sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nSo if mirrors and photos weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t get set off as much… but I think the concern would still be there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you had a more accurate perception of your body shape and weight?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. Maybe it would be a little easier if I saw myself more accurately, and I want to say it would fix it, but I’m not sure it would.\n\nBecause even when it’s just the scale going up a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had something with bread—I get this tense feeling right away. Then I sort of… compensate the next day, cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. Not in some extreme way, I don’t think, but enough that it’s hard to ignore.\n\nSo I guess the fear would still be there, even if my perception was better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight would decrease if you had a more realistic view of your body shape?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yeah. I think if I could see myself more realistically, it might take some of the edge off.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely stop the fear. Like, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense pretty fast. I don’t know, I’ve always thought I just looked kind of lean from work, being on ladders and moving around all day, long days and all that. But they seemed to think I should get it checked out, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of strong, I guess. But yeah, I do get pretty tense if the scale goes up even a little. Like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something heavier, I’ll notice it the next day and then I sort of cut back to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that probably sounds a bit much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I don’t really see it as unhealthy, exactly. I know my friends have made comments, and the doctor seemed concerned, so I’m not trying to ignore that. But I’m still getting up, going to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work like I always do. So part of me feels like maybe people are overreacting a bit? I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I… not really underweight, no. Or at least that’s not what jumps out at me. I know that might sound like I’m dodging it.\n\nWhen I see myself in a mirror, or in photos next to one of my murals, I’m usually looking at whether my stomach looks flat enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp… defined, I guess. And if it doesn’t, it can kind of throw off how I feel about the whole day. I know that’s probably not the best way to judge it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think what would count as “large.” Like, I might eat more than I planned at a dinner or something and then feel kind of uncomfortable about it, but not like… a lot of food in an hour, the way you’re describing. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt guilty or uncomfortable after eating more than I meant to, but I don’t do that. I know that’s probably something you have to ask, but… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a planned way, like “I ate this so now I have to burn it off.” I mean, my job can be pretty physical, and sometimes I’ll take a walk or try to keep moving if I feel kind of heavy after eating, but I wouldn’t say I’m exercising specifically to cancel out calories.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say it’s the most important thing, because that sounds… I don’t know, kind of shallow or bad. But it probably matters more to me than I want it to.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll end up judging the whole day by whether my stomach and my face look sharp enough. If they don’t, it just… it gets in my head. I know that probably isn’t the right way to measure things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight even if everyone around you told you that you looked healthy and didn't need to lose any more weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I would listen to them… especially if people were saying it in a caring way. But if I’m being honest, I don’t know that it would stop me.\n\nLike, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or if I had bread or something, I get really tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, but it’s hard for me to just trust that “healthy” means I don’t need to do anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what you actually see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. Like, I don’t look in the mirror and think, “I’m heavier than I really am,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck on certain parts—my stomach, my face—and whether they look sharp enough. If I catch myself in a mirror, or in a photo next to one of my murals, it can kind of decide how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably sounds a little much… but that’s more what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is distorted when you look in the mirror, different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely different from what other people see, because that sounds dramatic.\n\nBut when I catch myself in a mirror, or if I see a photo of me next to one of my murals, I really focus on my stomach and my face. Like whether they look sharp enough. And if they don’t, it can kind of ruin how I feel about the whole day. I know that probably isn’t a normal amount to put on one reflection.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to be as thin as possible, or is it more about not wanting to feel out of control with your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like… wanting to be as thin as humanly possible, exactly. That sounds extreme, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more that if my weight goes up even a little, I feel really unsettled. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve had bread or something, I’ll check and then get tense if the number is higher. Then the next day I’ll cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that’s probably not the healthiest way to handle it… but it feels like I need to get it back under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or upset if you couldn't maintain your current body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’d completely fall apart or anything, but if I felt like I couldn’t keep my shape where it is, I’d get pretty anxious.\n\nLike if the scale is up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense. Then the next day I usually try to cut back so it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds a bit rigid, but it’s more about feeling like I need to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about feeling like you're losing control over your body, rather than just not wanting to be a certain weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it. It’s not just the exact number, like I’m chasing some specific weight. It’s more that if the scale goes up even a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had bread—I get this tense feeling, like I’ve let something slip.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually cut back the next day, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that might sound a little overcontrolled, but… yeah, it feels more like needing to get my body back under control than just not wanting one particular number.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you didn't care about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say it wouldn’t matter if I didn’t care how I looked. That would probably be the healthier answer, right?\n\nBut honestly, a lot of it does come from seeing myself. Like if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll kind of judge how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach and face look… sharp enough, I guess. Not in a vain way, or at least I don’t think so, but it really affects me.\n\nSo… if I truly didn’t care how my body looked, maybe the need to control it wouldn’t be as strong. Which sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nSo if mirrors and photos weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t get set off as much… but I think the concern would still be there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you had a more accurate perception of your body shape and weight?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. Maybe it would be a little easier if I saw myself more accurately, and I want to say it would fix it, but I’m not sure it would.\n\nBecause even when it’s just the scale going up a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had something with bread—I get this tense feeling right away. Then I sort of… compensate the next day, cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. Not in some extreme way, I don’t think, but enough that it’s hard to ignore.\n\nSo I guess the fear would still be there, even if my perception was better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight would decrease if you had a more realistic view of your body shape?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yeah. Then the next day I’ll usually cut back a bit, just to make sure it doesn’t… stay. I know that probably sounds more intense than I mean it to, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to lose weight even if you were already underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t like saying yes, because it sounds kind of unreasonable.
