I don’t want to make it sound like I’d completely fall apart or anything, but if I felt like I couldn’t keep my shape where it is, I’d get pretty anxious.\n\nLike if the scale is up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense. Then the next day I usually try to cut back so it doesn’t… stick, I guess. I know that probably sounds a bit rigid, but it’s more about feeling like I need to keep it under control.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about feeling like you're losing control over your body, rather than just not wanting to be a certain weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably closer to it. It’s not just the exact number, like I’m chasing some specific weight. It’s more that if the scale goes up even a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had bread—I get this tense feeling, like I’ve let something slip.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually cut back the next day, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. I know that might sound a little overcontrolled, but… yeah, it feels more like needing to get my body back under control than just not wanting one particular number.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to control your weight if you didn't care about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say it wouldn’t matter if I didn’t care how I looked. That would probably be the healthier answer, right?\n\nBut honestly, a lot of it does come from seeing myself. Like if I catch myself in a mirror, or I see a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll kind of judge how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach and face look… sharp enough, I guess. Not in a vain way, or at least I don’t think so, but it really affects me.\n\nSo… if I truly didn’t care how my body looked, maybe the need to control it wouldn’t be as strong. But right now, yeah, the way I look is definitely tied into it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape would still be a concern for you if you couldn't see yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it would be less intense if I couldn’t actually see myself, yeah.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would go away completely. A lot of it starts when I catch myself in a mirror, or if there’s a photo of me next to one of my murals. I’ll sort of judge the whole day by whether my stomach and face look sharp enough. Which sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud, but that’s usually what happens.\n\nSo if mirrors and photos weren’t there, maybe I wouldn’t get set off as much… but I think the concern would still be there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you had a more accurate perception of your body shape and weight?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. Maybe it would be a little easier if I saw myself more accurately, and I want to say it would fix it, but I’m not sure it would.\n\nBecause even when it’s just the scale going up a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had something with bread—I get this tense feeling right away. Then I sort of… compensate the next day, cut back, just to make sure it doesn’t stick. Not in some extreme way, I don’t think, but enough that it’s hard to ignore.\n\nSo I guess the fear would still be there, even if my perception was better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight would decrease if you had a more realistic view of your body shape?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yeah. I think if I could see myself more realistically, it might take some of the edge off.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely stop the fear. Like, if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something, I get tense pretty fast. Then the next day I’ll usually cut back a bit, just to make sure it doesn’t… stay. I know that probably sounds more intense than I mean it to, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to lose weight even if you were already underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t like saying yes, because it sounds kind of unreasonable. But I think I probably would still try to bring it down, or at least stop it from going up.\n\nLike if the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner, or if I’ve had bread or something, I get tense. Then the next day I’ll cut back a bit, just so it doesn’t stick. I know that may not be the “right” answer, but that’s honestly what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your weight if you were at a weight that others considered healthy\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, if everyone else said it was a healthy weight, I’d want to believe that was enough.\n\nBut honestly, if the scale went up even a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had bread or something—I’d still get that tense feeling. Then the next day I’d probably cut back a bit, just to make sure it didn’t stick. Not anything dramatic, I don’t think, but… yeah, I’d still be trying to control it. Does that answer it okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
